content ud s̄c̄, these states provide unambiguous evidence of genuine tetraquark configurations. The existence of these
states was first confirmed in the D+ K − invariant-mass spectrum of the B− → D∗− D+ K − decay [212]. Theoretically, the
two states may be interpreted as compact quark-model tetraquark states [213–229], hadronic molecules [230–251],
including three-body hadronic bound states [252, 253], kinematically generated singularities arising from intermedi-
ate triangle diagrams [254], or threshold effects [255, 256]. In Ref. [257], ways to discriminate among interpretations
for the charmed–strange tetraquark states are outlined. In the compact tetraquark and molecular scenarios, the T cs     ∗0
                                     + −        0 0
is expected to decay into both D K and D K̄ final states with comparable rates under the assumption of isospin
symmetry. In contrast, in the kinematic-origin hypothesis, the T cs ∗0
                                                                       structure produced in the B− → D− T cs  ∗0
                                                                                                                  decay is
                                              + −                          0 0
predicted to couple more strongly to the D K final state than to the D K̄ one, this way offering a possible experi-
mental tool to discriminate among these interpretations. In any case, the absence of sibling states in the D∗ K̄ channels,
as predicted by approximate HQSS, poses a serious obstacle to their identification as predominantly DK̄ molecular
states.
     A recent amplitude analysis performed by the LHCb Collaboration on the B− → D− D0 K̄ 0 decay, where the K̄ 0
meson is reconstructed via the KS0 mass eigenstate, was designed to search for the T cs
                                                                                      ∗0
                                                                                         states [258]. The analysis uses
proton–proton collision data corresponding to an integrated luminosity of 9 fb collected at C.M. energies of 7, 8,
                                                                                −1

and 13 TeV. The baseline amplitude model, consisting solely of conventional D∗−   sJ resonances and nonresonant con-
tributions in the D− KS0 channel, provides an adequate description of the M(D− D0 ) distribution, but fails to reproduce
the M(D0 KS0 ) spectrum, particularly in the region around 2.9 GeV/c2 . Modifying the D∗−sJ model assumptions or intro-
ducing charmonium-like tetraquark candidates decaying to the D− D0 system does not improve the fit quality. A better
fit is obtained when including an additional resonant contribution in the D0 KS0 channel, modelled with a relativistic
BW function with floating mass and width, as shown in Fig. 18. The preferred solution corresponds to a spin-parity
assignment of J P = 0+ , yielding the fitted parameters

4.1.2. Observation of T c∗s̄0 (2900)0 and T c∗s̄0 (2900)++
    Motivated by the discoveries of the csūd̄ states, theoretical predictions were subsequently made for a doubly-
charged partner state with the quark composition [c s̄ud̄] and its isospin counterpart [c s̄ūd] [214, 222, 257, 259–261].
A dedicated search for such candidates was performed by the LHCb Collaboration using proton–proton collision data
corresponding to a total integrated luminosity of 9 fb−1 , collected at C.M. energies of 7, 8, and 13 TeV. The analysis
was based on the decay modes B0 → D̄0 D+s π− and B+ → D− D+s π+ , for which a combined amplitude analysis was
carried out to explore the resonant structure across the Dalitz plot [262].
    Initial amplitude models included conventional contributions from excited D̄∗ states decaying to D̄0 π− and D− π+ ,
described by relativistic BW functions, together with the D̄π S -wave parameterized using the quasi-model-independent
approach [263]. While these components provided a good description of most invariant mass projections, except for
the structure near 2.9 GeV/c2 in the D+s π spectrum. Attempts to introduce additional D̄∗ resonances with freely vary-
ing spin hypotheses did not resolve the discrepancy. An improved description of the data was achieved only after
introducing two new exotic states, denoted T c∗s̄0 (2900)0 and T c∗s̄0 (2900)++ , decaying to D+s π− and D+s π+ , respectively.
Under the assumption of isospin symmetry, their masses, widths, and complex couplings were constrained to be com-
mon parameters across the two decay channels. The M(D+s π) distributions of the fit results are shown in Fig. 19. The
statistical significance of T c∗s̄0 (2900) contributions exceeds 9σ, establishing these structures as compelling candidates
for doubly charged and neutral open-charm tetraquarks.
       The mass and width of the new states are measured to be (2.909 ± 0.010) GeV/c2 and (0.134 ± 0.019) GeV,
respectively. Their quantum numbers are determined to be J P = 0+ . An alternative amplitude model in which the
T c∗s̄0 (2900)0 and T c∗s̄0 (2900)++ states are allowed to have independent resonance parameters yields results consistent
with the isospin-symmetric hypothesis. These structures represent a new class of open-charm tetraquark candidates
containing a c quark and an s̄ antiquark. The measured mass of the T c∗s̄0 (2900) is compatible with that of the previously
reported T cs0∗
                 (2870)0 , also assigned J P = 0+ and observed in the D+ K − final state [210, 211]. However, their widths
and flavor compositions differ, indicating that they may correspond to distinct configurations within the spectrum of
open-charm exotic hadrons.

4.1.3. Properties of D∗s0 (2317)+ and D s1 (2460)+
    In the standard quark model scheme, open-charm mesons are conventionally assigned the name D and are usually
identified by their charge or, more rarely, explicitly by their light-quark content,

                                                  D0 ≡ Du ∼ cū,         D+ ≡ Dd ∼ cd̄,                                      D̄0 ≡ D̄u ∼ uc̄,     D− ≡ D̄d ∼ dc̄.                                               (19)

In this family, the ground state and its first excitation are the pseudoscalar and vector mesons D and D∗ , respectively,
where in both cases the orbital angular momentum in the quark–antiquark pair is l = 0 and the total spin of the pair is
                                                                                                                        28

Figure 19: Distributions of (a) M(D+s π− ) of B0 → D̄0 D+s π− decays; and (b) M(D+s π+ ) for the B+ → D− D+s π+ sample. The data are overlaid with
the fit results with the inclusion of the new 0+ D+s π resonant states [262].


S = 0 (for D) or S = 1 (for D∗ ). Next in the spectrum of excitations of the cq̄ (with q = u, d) mesons come positive-
parity states D J , with J = 0, 1, 2, which are sums of the angular momentum l = 1 and the spin of the quark–antiquark
pair S = 0 or S = 1. As a result, one has a quartet of P-level states: one scalar D∗0 , two axial vectors D1 , and one
tensor D∗2 , where we resort to the standard PDG notations [24]. Since the charmed quark is much heavier than the u
and d quarks, HQET applies to these mesons. In particular, it is easy to see that, in the gedanken limit of an infinitely
heavy charmed quark, mc → ∞, the quartet above naturally splits into two degenerate doublets corresponding to
 jl = 1/2 and jl = 3/2 (therefore, often denoted as D1/2 and D3/2 ), with sq = 12 and jl = l + sq for the spin and total
momentum of the light degrees of freedom, respectively. Once the charmed quark mass decreases from infinity to
its physical value, the masses of the D J mesons acquire 1/mc corrections that stem from spin-dependent interactions
and, therefore, the four D J ’s split from each other. Since quark–antiquark mesons composed of different quarks are
not eigenstates of the C-parity operator, the two resulting physical D1 mesons can be understood as certain dynamical
(that is, not constrained by any symmetry) mixtures of the 1 P1 and 3 P1 cq̄ terms. The corresponding mixing angle
can in principle be evaluated in the framework of a particular quark model once its parameters are fixed to the data
— there exists a vast amount of papers on the subject. In particular, one of the questions broadly discussed in the
literature is the ordering of the physical heavy-light meson levels since different models predict it differently.
    One of the most distinct predictions of HQET for the D J mesons is the hierarchy of their total widths. The states
belonging to the D1/2 doublet can decay to the D(∗) π final states in the S wave, so they must be broad. On the contrary,
the states belonging to the D3/2 doublet decay to these final states in the D wave, so their total widths are expected
to be relatively small. Indeed, the outlined pattern takes place in nature since the D∗0 (2300) and D1 (2430) mesons
possess the widths of the order of 200–300 MeV while the widths of the D1 (2420) and D∗2 (2460) mesons are as small
as only 30–50 MeV [24]. At first glance, the simple (and somewhat naïve — see below) picture just drawn equally
applies to the generic positive-parity D sJ mesons with J = 0, 1, 2 and the quark content c s̄ given that the required
mass hierarchy, m s ≪ mc , does hold for them as well. However, the situation with these two charmed–strange
mesons appears to be more tricky than that with their nonstrange cousins. Indeed, already the first observation of
the D∗s0 (2317)+ made by BaBar in 2003 in the final state D+s π0 [264] attracted a lot of attention to this meson since
the quark model had predicted its mass at around 2.48 GeV/c2 [265], which is about 160 MeV/c2 higher than the
experimentally observed value. To resolve the puzzle one needs to take into account the following arguments. The
phenomenon of spontaneous breaking of chiral symmetry in the vacuum of QCD entails the existence of eight light
pseudo-Goldstone bosons in the spectrum of theory: three pions, four kaons, and one η-meson. All above states are
supposed to become massless in the strict chiral limit of mu = md = m s = 0. However, while the masses of the u
and d quarks are indeed tiny on the typical hadronic scale, the mass of the s quark is not. As a result, in the physical
world, the kaons appear to be noticeably heavier than the pions and the D(∗) K thresholds lie higher than the D(∗) π
thresholds by approximately mK − mπ ≃ 350 MeV/c2 . The latter observation implies that the physical D∗s0 (2317)
and D s1 (2460) mesons reside not far (within just ≃ 30–40 MeV/c2 ) from the DK and D∗ K thresholds, respectively.
Therefore, a strong influence of the corresponding two-hadron channels on the nature of these two D sJ mesons has
to be anticipated and taken into account — see, for example, Ref. [266] and references therein for a discussion of the
                                                                                                                29
D∗s0 (2317) and D s1 (2460) mesons as hadronic molecules rather than generic c s̄ states. In particular, a strong coupling
to the DK channel allows one to understand the position of the D∗s0 (2317) pole on the complex energy plane [267].
No surprise that the internal structure of the D∗s0 (2317)+ and D s1 (2460)+ mesons has been under active debate since
their discovery in 2003 [268–277].
     The predominantly molecular nature of the D∗s0 (2317)+ and D s1 (2460)+ resonances has received a strong and
consistent theoretical support over the last decade [119, 278, 279]. A key step in this direction was provided in
Ref. [278], where D(∗) –ϕ (with ϕ for Goldstone bosons) scattering in the J P = 0+ channel and in the (S , I) = (0, 1/2)
sector was investigated. The analysis revealed compelling evidence for a two-pole structure in the D∗0 (2300) region,
which was subsequently corroborated by the excellent agreement between parameter-free predictions of low-lying
finite-volume energy levels obtained from next-to-leading-order (NLO) unitarized heavy-meson chiral perturbation
theory (HMChPT) amplitudes [280] and LQCD determinations [281]. Additional LQCD support was reported in
Ref. [282], where the same unitarized NLO HMChPT amplitudes were shown to successfully reproduce the 0+ and
1+ charmed–strange finite-volume spectra obtained in Ref. [283]. The latter lattice study explicitly incorporated the
nearby DK and D∗ K thresholds via four-quark interpolating operators, providing further evidence for the molecu-
lar interpretation of the D∗s0 (2317) and D s1 (2460) states. A detailed analysis of the light-flavor SU(3) structure of
the D–ϕ interaction clarified the dynamical origin of the two-pole structure [278], identifying the lower pole as the
SU(3) partner of the D∗s0 (2317)+ . This naturally implies a hadronic molecular interpretation for the corresponding
non-strange state as well. Analogous patterns were later observed in the J P = 1+ axial channel and in the bot-
tom sector, suggesting that such structures are a generic consequence of the D(∗) –ϕ chiral dynamics. These findings
offer a natural resolution to a long-standing puzzle in charm spectroscopy, namely the unexpectedly high masses
of the non-strange D∗0 (2300) and D1 (2430) mesons relative to their strange counterparts. Moreover, the molecu-
lar picture provides a coherent explanation for two additional puzzles: the unusually low masses of the D∗s0 (2317)
and D s1 (2460) compared to quark-model expectations, and the striking near equality (within about 2 MeV) between
the D s1 (2460)–D∗s0 (2317) mass splitting and the hyperfine splitting between the ground-state vector D∗+ and pseu-
doscalar D+ mesons. Further support for this molecular framework was obtained in Refs. [279, 284], where the chiral
amplitudes constrained in Ref. [278] were shown to be fully consistent with high-quality experimental data on the
B− → D+ π− π− and B0s → D̄0 K − π+ decay channels measured by the LHCb Collaboration [263, 285]. Taken together,
these results point toward a paradigm shift in heavy–light meson spectroscopy, challenging the traditional constituent
qq̄ interpretation and highlighting the central role of hadronic molecular components and coupled-channel chiral dy-
namics [279]. Overall, these developments strongly support the existence of a two-pole structure in the D∗0 (2300)
region and an SU(3)-driven pattern in the 0+ and 1+ heavy–light sectors [278]. They establish a coherent framework
in which coupled-channel dynamics and chiral symmetry play a central role in shaping the isoscalar scalar and axial
heavy–light meson spectrum [279]. Subsequent theoretical studies have further explored the implications of chiral
symmetry for scalar charmed mesons [284, 286], consolidating this emerging molecular-driven perspective. In addi-
tion, Ref. [287] discussed whether the two-pole structure observed for the D∗0 (2300) can be understood from lattice
studies of Dπ scattering at different pion masses [281], where only one pole was reported in the D∗0 channel, and
Ref. [288] examined the role played by the D∗0 (2100) in B → Dπℓν semileptonic decays. In this context, it is also
worth mentioning that Ref. [289] showed that the LQCD data for the D → Kℓν semileptonic decay were compatible
with the D∗s0 (2317) state, while the LQCD scalar form factor of the D → πℓν transition was used in that work to
predict the existence of an I = 1/2 S -wave resonance at (2.2 ± 0.1) GeV/c2 , compatible with the lower state found in
unitarized chiral studies.
     The total widths of the D∗s0 (2317) and D s1 (2460) are small (appear at a 1 MeV level or less) since they mainly
decay through the isospin-breaking hadronic channels D∗s0 (2317)+ → D+s π0 and D s1 (2460)+ → D∗+                0
                                                                                                             s π , respec-
tively. Note also that different assignments for these mesons result in different predictions for their decay patterns
— for instance, for the ratios of their partial radiative and (isospin breaking) one-pion decay widths B(D∗s0 (2317) →
D∗s γ)/B(D∗s0 (2317) → D s π) and B(D s1 (2460) → D s γ)/B(D s1 (2460) → D∗s π). For evaluation of these ratios in the
molecular model for both D∗s0 (2317) and D s1 (2460) mesons see Refs. [290, 291] and Refs. [292, 293] for recent up-
dates. In Ref. [294], the three-body radiative decays D s1 (2460) → γDK are argued to be able to provide additional
insight into the nature of the D s1 (2460) meson and allow one to assess the admixture of the D∗ K molecule in its wave
function. The above decays have also been comprehensively studied in the chiral doublet model under the assumption
of the D∗s0 (2317) and D s1 (2460) being chiral partners of the pseudoscalar D s and vector D∗s mesons, respectively [270].

A reanalysis of the existing experimental data on the radiative decays of the D s1 (2536) — a would-be J = 1 quark-
model sibling of the D s1 (2460) — is performed in Ref. [295]. In Ref. [296], the radiative decays D∗s0 (2317) → D∗s γ
and D s1 (2460) → D s γ are studied by employing the QCD sum rule technique. In Refs. [297–299], the widths of the
radiative decays D s1 (2460) → D(∗)                         (∗)
                                   s γ and D s1 (2536) → D s γ are calculated, with relativistic corrections taken into
account, assuming all involved charm-strange mesons to be conventional quark–antiquark states. In Ref. [300], the
decays D s1 (2460) → D(∗)s γ are studied including both c s̄ and two-hadron components. Interestingly, as argued in
Refs. [301–303], the shape of the π+ π− invariant mass distribution in the di-pion decay D s1 → D s ππ may shed light
on the nature of the decaying meson since a double-hump structure is expected for the D s1 as a D∗ K molecule versus
a single-hump structure for a generic c s̄ meson. Electromagnetic properties of the D s1 (2460) and D s1 (2536) mesons
as hadronic molecules are studied in Ref. [304] by employing the QCD sum rules technique.
    The decay D∗s0 (2317)+ → D∗+  s γ was searched for by CLEO, Belle, √ and BaBar, but no signals were found [264,
306, 307]. Using large data samples of 980.4 fb−1 and 427.9 fb−1 at s around 10.6 GeV, Belle and Belle II updated
this branching fraction ratio measurement in the continuum e+ e− → cc̄ process [305]. Figure 20 shows the invariant

      The comparison between this branching-fraction ratio and various theoretical predictions is shown in Fig. 21,
adapted from a recent Belle paper [305]. The measured ratio is generally larger than the predictions obtained for a
predominantly molecular D∗s0 (2317)+ state [290, 292, 293, 308], while remaining below the expectation for a pure
c s̄ state in conventional quark models [268]. Belle (II) further argues that the predictions of Refs. [270, 309], which
are also classified there as quark-model results, are consistent with the measurement under the assumption that the
D∗s0 (2317)+ is a pure c s̄ state. From the theoretical perspective, however, this conclusion should be interpreted with
caution. Indeed, within the c s̄ assignment for the D∗s0 (2317)+ , Ref. [309] predicts a radiative width Γ(D∗s0 (2317) →
                                                                                                                  +
D∗+s γ) ≈ (17.1 ± 3.9) keV, which exceeds the estimate for the total width of a conventional c s̄ D s0 (2317) state,
                                                                                                         ∗

Γtot (D s0 (2317)) ≈ 7 keV, obtained in Ref. [269]. Such a comparison suggests a significant tension between these
         ∗

predictions. Furthermore, the results of Ref. [270] were obtained within a specific framework combining heavy-
quark symmetry with a particular realization of chiral symmetry for heavy mesons. Consequently, they should not be
regarded as predictions of a generic quark model.
     A possible interpretation of the Belle (II) result in Ref. [305] is that the D∗s0 (2317)+ could be an admixture of
pure c s̄ and molecular state, which was suggested in Refs. [282, 310–312]. Belle and Belle II are performing the
measurement of the ratio of branching fractions for D s1 (2460)+ → D∗+                        +        ∗+ 0
                                                                          s γ and D s1 (2460) → D s π to help under-
                                    +                                       + −               +       + − +
stand the nature of the D s1 (2460) . The invariant mass distribution of π π in D s1 (2460) → π π D s is also being
investigated to distinguish a pure D∗ K molecular state and a compact state model for the D s1 (2460)+ . In B decays,
using the recoil mass technique, the absolute branching fractions for B0 → D∗s0 (2317)+ D− and B0 → D s1 (2460)+ D−
can be measured in the recoil mass spectrum of D− . Further, the absolute branching fractions for D∗s0 (2317)+ and
D s1 (2460)+ decaying into different final states can be determined. With large data samples anticipated at Belle II in
the near future, the above absolute branching fractions are expected to be achieved. Complementary precision studies
of the D sJ mesons performed in the e+ e− annihilation in the ongoing experiment BESIII may potentially constitute
an essential addition to its future physics program [313]. The first successful steps in this direction have already been
reported recently [314].

                                                         f0 (500)        474 ± 30 ± 18           224 ± 23 ± 16          248 +40

               f0 (500) + K-matrix T c s̄ (0+ )
                                                        T c++     0
                                                            s̄ /T c s̄   2327 ± 13 ± 13           96 ± 16 ± 23          156 +27

first tested models containing only known ππ states. Although these models describe the data statistically well, they
require implausibly large contributions from the f2 (1270) and f0 (980) resonances despite the limited phase space, and
exhibit unusually strong interference, indicating they may not reflect the true decay dynamics. To further investigate
the decay model, additional exotic contributions decaying to D+s π± , denoted as T c++          0
                                                                                       s̄ and T c s̄ , were introduced under
                                                        P    +
isospin symmetry constraints. Models including a J = 0 T c s̄ resonance provide a fit quality comparable to models
employing only ππ resonances, as shown in Fig. 22. The T c s̄ contribution was parametrized using both relativistic BW
and K-matrix approaches. These parametrisations yield consistent mass values, whereas the extracted widths show
substantial model dependence, as shown in Table 5. The masses obtained using the K-matrix parametrisation agree
with the expectation in Ref. [316], where the T c s̄ states are interpreted as members of an isotriplet. However, both
the fitted mass and width deviate from the values reported in Ref. [317]. The distributions in Fig. 22 are consistent
with the results of Ref. [303] that contains further development of the ideas previously published in Ref. [301]. In
theoretical work [302], the di-pion transition D s1 (2460) → D s π+ π− was investigated under the assumption of a molec-
ular D s1 (2460) built mostly from the D∗ K and D∗s η components. In the same picture, the D s1 (2536) is then argued to
contain a dominant DK ∗ molecular component, which results in specific predictions for the mass distributions in the
decays of this state [318]. Thus, the entire approach and particular conclusions and predictions of Refs. [302, 318] can
be critically assessed as soon as experimental data on the two-pion decays of D s1 (2536) become available at LHCb.
On the contrary, only a little molecular admixture (at the level of 2%) in the wave function of the D s1 (2536) is ob-
tained in Ref. [319], where this meson comes out as a predominantly quark–antiquark state as based on the approach
previously suggested in Ref. [320]. Thus, further experimental studies of the state D s1 (2536) and, in particular, the
decay modes most sensitive to its nature would be allow one to shed more light on its internal structure and draw
certain conclusions concerning the quartet of the positive-parity states D sJ .

4.2. Hidden charm tetraquarks
4.2.1. Observation of T cc̄1 (4430)+
    The first evidence for the existence of a charged charmonium-like state T cc̄1 (4430)+ (also known as Zc (4430)+ ),
with a minimal quark content of cc̄ud̄, was obtained in the ψ(2S )π+ final state of the decay B → ψ(2S )Kπ [12, 321].
The amplitude analysis has established its spin–parity quantum numbers to be J P = 1+ . Despite extensive theo-
retical and experimental studies, the underlying nature of the T cc̄1 (4430)+ structure remains unresolved. Dynamical
interpretations generally treat the T cc̄1 (4430)+ structure as a genuine exotic state such as hadrocharmonium [322],
compact tetraquark [323], or a hadronic molecule [324–326]. In Refs. [327–330], it is argued to be an effect of
the D̄∗0 D+ –ψ(2S )π+ re-scatterings. In the molecular picture [324–326], the T cc̄1 (4430)+ is typically interpreted as
an S -wave D∗ D1 bound state with possible spin-parity assignments J P = 0− , 1− , 2− ; however, all of these assign-
ments are incompatible with the LHCb measurement. The compact tetraquark scenario predicts an octet of related
states [331–333], none of which have been conclusively observed, although the T cc̄1 (4430)+ is a possible candidate.
The kinematical interpretations attribute the observed structure to rescattering effects in B-meson decays, which can
reproduce the measured properties without introducing a genuine resonance [327–330]. At present, none of the pro-
posed scenarios provides a fully consistent description of the T cc̄1 (4430)+ .
    Recently, the LHCb Collaboration has performed an amplitude analysis of the B+ → ψ(2S )KS0 π+ decay using pp
                                                                                                         +
collision data collected during the period 2016–2018 [334]. The analysis shows that the inclusion of a T cc̄ is required
                         +                                                                        +
to describe the ψ(2S )π invariant mass spectrum, in particular in the region 4.2 < M(ψ(2S )π ) < 4.7 GeV/c2 .

4.2.2. Possible consequence for the B+ → ψ(2S )K + π+ π−
        The decay B+ → ψ(2S )K + π+ π− provides a rich environment to search for a variety of exotic hadron contributions.
Owing to its multi-body topology, this channel is sensitive to charged charmonium-like states T cc̄ , previously observed
                − +
in B0 → T cc̄    K → [ψ(2S )π− ]K + decays [321, 336, 337], as well as to strange partners T cc̄          +                 +
                                                                                                            s̄ reported in B →
  +                +                                                                                                  + −
T cc̄ s̄ ϕ → [J/ψK ]ϕ decays [338]. In addition, contributions from neutral exotic states decaying to ψ(2S )π π such as
X 0 → ψ(2S )π+ π− , or from T cc̄
                               0               + −
                                  s̄ → ψ(2S )K π are also possible. The decay topology further allows for more complex
                                             0
exotic cascade processes, for example X → [T cc̄   −
                                                      → ψ(2S )π− ]π+ . The LHCb Collaboration has recently reported an
                                       +           + + −
amplitude analysis of the decay B → ψ(2S )K π π using the full Run 1 and Run 2 data sets [339]. The baseline
model, comprising 53 interfering amplitudes, provides an adequate description of the data. A rich spectrum of exotic
contributions is included in the fit: four X 0 → ψ(2S )π+ π− candidates, three charged T cc̄  ±
                                                                                                    → ψ(2S )π± structures, one
  +                +                                 0             + −
T cc̄ s̄ → ψ(2S )K component, and three neutral T cc̄ s̄ → ψ(2S )K π candidates. The masses and widths of the exotic
states are treated as free parameters in the fit, with the exception of T cc̄1
                                                                          ∗
                                                                               (4055)+ and T cc̄ s̄1 (4000)+ , whose parameters
are fixed due to limited experimental sensitivity.
      The dominant exotic contribution in the baseline model is T cc̄0 ∗
                                                                         (4475)0 , which is found to decay predominantly
through the ψ(2S )ρ(770) channel. Its fitted mass is (4475 ± 7 ± 12) MeV/c2 , compatible with the χc0 (4500) structure
                             0

previously reported in B+ → χc0 (4500)(→ J/ψϕ)K + [338]. However, its BW width, (231 ± 19 ± 32) MeV, is signifi-
cantly larger than the reported Γ = (77+12 −10 ) MeV of the χc0 (4500). Assuming isospin conservation, one could interpret
T∗cc̄0 (4475)0 and χc0 (4500) as different states with I = 1 (cc̄(uū − dd̄)) and I = 0 (cc̄s s̄), respectively; yet under this
hypothesis, the expected mass difference would be much larger than the observed difference. The T cc̄1 (4650)0 and
T cc̄0
  ∗
       (4710)0 states are found to be compatible, within uncertainties, with the χc1 (4685) and χc0 (4700) resonances previ-
ously reported in B+ → J/ψϕ K + decays (see Table 6). Given that these final states carry different isospin, identifying
the corresponding pairs as the same physical states would require significant isospin-violating effects, for which no
compelling mechanism has yet been established. Besides direct production in B decays, charged hidden-charm exotic
                                                                                                                     0        − +
states are also produced via cascade decays of higher-mass exotic resonances, such as T cc̄    ∗

Argand diagrams for the dominant components — T cc̄0        ∗
                                                               → ψ(2S )π+ π− , T cc̄0              + −
                                                                                      s̄1 → ψ(2S )K π , and T cc̄1 → ψ(2S )π —
                                                                                                              ±             ±

are presented in Fig. 24. In each case, a characteristic counter-clockwise trajectory is observed, exhibiting phase mo-
tion consistent with the expectation for a resonance. Details of other components are available in Ref. [339]. Despite
high statistical significances of all reported signals, the broad line shapes and the complexity of the amplitude model
— comprising 53 interfering components — introduce substantial model dependence, which complicates a precise
determination of the underlying nature of these structures.

4.2.3. Radiative decays of X(3872)
    The photon emission vertex is described by Quantum Electrodynamics that is the most studied and well understood
field theory entering the Standard Model. For this reason, the radiative decays of hadrons attract special attention
of both experimentalists and theorists and are often regarded as a strong investigation tool to probe the nature and
properties of various hadronic systems. Since the X(3872) definitely contains a cc̄ pair, its radiative decays to hidden-
and open-charm final states have been under active studies for many years.
    The LHCb Collaboration has recently reported a measurement of the radiative decays of the X(3872) into the
γψ(2S ) and γJ/ψ final states, using pp collision data collected during Run 1 and Run 2, corresponding to a total
integrated luminosity of 9 fb−1 [342]. The analysis is based on the decay chain B+ → X(3872)(→ γψ)K + , where ψ
denotes either J/ψ or ψ(2S ) reconstructed through their µ+ µ− decay modes. A signal significance of 4.8σ and 6.0σ

                                                                           36
                        Table 7: Experimental measurements of the ratio RX in Eq. (21) for the X(3872) radiative decays.
              BaBaR [346]          Belle [343]        LHCb (old) [347]           BESIII [348]             LHCb (new) [342]
                 3.4 ± 1.4             < 2.1         2.46 ± 0.64 ± 0.29              < 0.59          1.67 ± 0.21 ± 0.12 ± 0.04


for the decay X(3872) → γψ(2S ) is obtained in the Run 1 and Run 2 data sets, respectively, constituting the first
observation of this radiative transition. The ratio of the partial radiative widths,

                                                               B(X(3872) → γψ(2S ))
                                                       RX =                         ,                                                          (21)
                                                                B(X(3872) → γJ/ψ)
is measured to be
                                                   RLHCb
                                                    X    = 1.67 ± 0.21 ± 0.12 ± 0.04,                                                          (22)
for the combined Run 1 and Run 2 data.
    Belle II is also performing the measurement of RX . In the previous measurement using Belle data only, the
X(3872) → γJ/ψ was observed with a significance of 5.5σ including systematic uncertainties. But no significant
X(3872) → γψ(2S ) signal was found [343]. In this updated work on X(3872) → γψ(2S ) in B± → X(3872)K ± and
B0 → X(3872)K 0 , a Boosted Decision Tree (BDT) using the XGBoost algorithm is employed to suppress the dominant
B±,0 → ψ(2S )K ∗±,0 background. Variables such as M(π0 ), M(K ∗±,0 ), the recoil mass of the kaon, the mass difference
between the B and K mesons, the kaon momentum, and the polar angle between K and γ are used. By applying
the BDT classifier requirement, the background rejection rate is approximately 80% with a high signal efficiency
of 80%. Belle II performed a sensitivity study of observing X(3872) → γψ(2S ) by using 5,000 sets of simulated
pseudoexperiments. In this study, the signal yield for X(3872) → γψ(2S ) is determined under an assumption of
RX = 1.67 [342]. The background Monte Carlo (MC) samples including e+ e− → B+ B− and e+ e− → B0 B̄0 inclusive
decay events with the same integrated luminosity as the data are used. Here, the integrated luminosities at Belle and
Belle II are 711 fb−1 and 363 fb−1 , respectively. The mean statistical significance is 3.2σ for X(3872) → γψ(2S ) from
5,000 sets of simulated pseudoexperiments. With the large data samples collected at Belle II in the near future, it is
promising to observe the X(3872) → γψ(2S ) signal.
    The experimental progress in measuring the ratio RX from Eq. (21) over the years is summarized in Table 7, and
a comparison of the results is shown in Fig. 25. Noticeably, the recent LHCb result in Eq. (22) lies below the upper
limit set by the Belle Collaboration and is consistent with earlier measurements from BaBar and the previous LHCb
analysis. Meanwhile, it still demonstrates a significant tension7 with the strong upper limit reported by the BESIII
Collaboration8 . Thus, the most recent LHCb result in Eq. (22) [342] is conclusive and establishes RX ≃ 1..2 that calls
for a theoretical interpretation.
    It is claimed in an early theoretical work [349] that the ratio in Eq. (21) is strongly sensitive to the nature of the
X(3872). In particular, RX ∼ 1 was found to be consistent with the X(3872) as a compact quark state. Indeed, various
quark model calculations demonstrate that such ratio around unity can be naturally gained for the X(3872) assigned
as a generic charmonium (see, for example, recent works [350, 351] and further results and papers quoted therein) or
a compact tetraquark [352]. In the meantime, a tiny ratio RmolX ≪ 1 was argued in Ref. [349] to be inevitable for the
X(3872) as a DD̄∗ molecule. This claim as well as the conclusion of the experimentally measured ratio RX in Table 7
being at odds with the molecular interpretation of the X(3872) is further repeatedly stressed in some recent works
[352, 353]. This conclusion is scrutinized in Ref. [354] where it is explained that in fact the radiative decays of the
molecular X(3872) to charmonia proceed via D(∗) -meson loops with the photon emitted by a D(∗) meson inside the
loop rather than through the light-quark annihilation mechanism suggested in Refs. [349, 352, 353]. Then, according
to the numerical estimates in Ref. [354], even a purely DD̄∗ molecule assignment for the X(3872) is consistent with

   7 In a recent theoretical work [344], this tension is explained by the contribution of a predicted 2−+ charmonium candidate, η , residing slightly
                                                                                                                                 c2
above the D∗0 D̄0 threshold.
   8 A recent concise review and prospects concerning the study of the X(3872), including its radiative decays, in the BESIII experiment can be

found in Ref. [345].

                                                                    BaBar                      2008 [346]
                                                                    Belle                      2011 [343]
                                                                    LHCb/Run 1 2014 [347]
                                                                    BESIII                     2020 [348]


                                                                    LHCb/Run 1 2024
                                                                    LHCb/Run 2 2024

                         0      1     2      3      4     5
                                          RX

Figure 25: Summary of the experimental results for the ratio of the partial decay widths in Eq. (21). Adapted from Ref. [342]. The results from
Ref. [342] for the Run 1 and Run 2 data sets are shown as red points with error bars. The colored band corresponds to the average of the LHCb
results in Ref. [342]. For the LHCb measurements, the outer error bars represent the total uncertainties, while the inner ones indicate the statistical
uncertainties only.


the ratio of the order of unity in Eq. (21) under a not restrictive assumption for the couplings of the vector charmonia
J/ψ and ψ(2S ) to the D(∗) mesons being numerically close to each other. Thus, the estimates of the ratio Rmol   X  ≪1
in Refs. [349, 352], based on the overlap of the molecular X(3872) wave function, do not apply to the studied case
and as such can not be regarded as decisive (see also a detailed discussion in Ref. [355]). Furthermore, the authors
of Ref. [354] conclude on a strong sensitivity of the ratio RX in Eq. (21) to the short-range physics implying that it is
mainly governed by the compact component of the X(3872) wave function. The size of this component, traditionally
measured in terms of the Weinberg field renormalisation factor Z [356], is estimated in many theoretical works to vary
from small values of the order 10% or less (see, for example, Refs. [147, 357]) to larger admixtures at the level of
20% (see, for example, Ref. [358]) and up to as large values as Z ≃ 0.5 (see, for example, Refs. [359, 360]). In either
case, given a strong sensitivity of RX to this short-range component established in Ref. [354], even a little admixture
of a compact state in the X(3872) wave function, irrespective of its particular nature, leads to an improved description
of the radiative decays and readily brings the ratio RX to the experimental value [130, 147, 354, 361]. In particular,
the one-node radial wave function of a generic χc1 (2P) charmonium state naturally exhibits a larger overlap with the
radial wave function of the ψ(2S ), which also has one node, than with the nodeless radial wave function of the J/ψ.
Therefore, values of the ratio RX exceeding unity arise naturally if the short-range component of the X(3872) wave
function is dominated by a cc̄ configuration. Furthermore, the presence of a short-range component in the X(3872)
wave function resolves the issue with a low probability of its prompt production in high-energy hadronic collisions
directly through the delocalized molecular component. This relevant worry was put forward in Refs. [362, 363] and
then addressed in Refs. [364, 365] from the molecular model perspective. It should also be noted in conclusion
that, given the confirmed total spin of the X(3872), J = 1, its two-photon decays are forbidden by the Landau-Yang
theorem.
    Further information of various models for the X(3872) and their compliance with the existing experimental data
can be found in the dedicated reviews on the X(3872) (see, for example, recent ones [353, 366]) as well as general
reviews on hadronic exotics (see, for example, Refs. [21, 119, 125, 367–369]). For a compilation of various theoretical
results for the ratio in Eq. (21) and the corresponding references see Table 1 in Ref. [342].

are from simulated events, not from the real data.


4.2.4. Pionic transitions from X(3872)
     The pionic transitions from the X(3872) to χcJ (J = 0, 1, 2) are expected to provide a sensitive probe of different
theoretical interpretations for the X(3872). Although these decay rates are expected to be very small for a conventional
cc̄ state hypothesis [370], they can be sizeable if the X(3872) is a tetraquark or hadronic molecule [370, 371]. The
ratios Γ0 : Γ1 : Γ2 of the decay widths (Γ0 = Γ(X(3872) → π0 χc0 ), Γ1 = Γ(X(3872) → π0 χc1 ), and Γ2 = Γ(X(3872) →
π0 χc2 )) are predicted by different theoretical models [370–374], which offer a complementary probe to discriminate
among different scenarios for X(3872). Previously, only the transition to J = 1 state was observed by BESIII [375],
and no significant signals were seen by BESIII and Belle for the other two modes [376, 377]. Recently the X(3872) →
π0 χcJ and X(3915) → π0 χc1 decays were searched for in B+ → π0 χcJ K + decays using a total data sample of 1.2 ab−1
in Belle and Belle II collected at the Υ(10580) resonance. The χc0 candidates are constructed using π+ π− , K + K − ,
and K + K − K + K − events, and the χc1,c2 candidates are constructed using γJ/ψ(→ ℓ+ ℓ− ) ones. Figure 26 shows the
invariant mass distributions of M(π0 χcJ ) for all J summed up and J = 0, 1, 2, respectively, in mock data sample.
The mock data consists of generic MC and signal MC samples. The generic MC samples, including e+ e− → B+ B−
and e+ e− → B0 B̄0 inclusive decay events, are scaled to the integrated luminosity of the data. The X(3872) and
X(3915) signal yields are generated by assuming the ratios of branching fractions B(X(3872) → π0 χc1 )/B(X(3872) →
π+ π− J/ψ) = 3.6, 0.88, 1.1 for J = 0, 1, 2 [375, 377], respectively, and B(B+ → X(3915)K + ) × B(X(3915) → π0 χc1 ) =
B(B+ → X(3872)K + ) × B(X(3872) → π+ π− J/ψ), with the previous Belle measurement of B(B+ → X(3872)K + ) ×
B(X(3872) → π+ π− J/ψ) = 8.63 × 10−6 [378] as input. A simultaneous fit to the mass spectra is performed to obtain
the signal yields. We perform 103 sets of simulated pseudoexperiments with the above configuration. The fitted
results in Fig. 26 represent the average signal yields from 103 sets of simulated pseudoexperiments. In Fig. 26, clear
X(3872) → π0 χcJ and X(3872) → π0 χc0 signals are observed, with the significances larger than 6.5σ and 6.3σ,

                                                                                       39
respectively, with systematic uncertainties included. No significant signals with systematic significance larger than
3σ are found from other modes. Thus, if the above assumed ratios are correct, with the currently available Belle and
Belle II data, it is promising to observe the X(3872) → π0 χcJ decay.
    Further studies of the di-pion transitions from the X(3872) may also provide valuable information on the nature of
the latter and reveal its possible partners. In particular, it is argued in Ref. [379] that a comparative study of the ππJ/ψ
subsystem produced in the decays B → KππJ/ψ between the neutral and the charged B’s may show a signal of the
isovector partner of the X(3872), advocated to exist in Ref. [380]. Furthermore, the predictions of the molecular and
compact tetraquark models for the isospin partner of the X(3872) considerably differ from each other thus providing
an additional option to probe the nature of the X(3872) [379].

4.2.5. The absolute branching fraction of X(3872) → π+ π− J/ψ
    In B-factory experiments, the absolute branching fraction of X(3872) → π+ π− J/ψ can be measured. The Υ(10580)
almost exclusively decays into a BB̄ pair. By exclusively reconstructing one B and identifying the K + in the decay
of the other B meson, one can obtain the branching fraction for B+ → K + X(3872). The product of the branching
fraction of B+ → K + X(3872) and the branching fraction of X(3872) → π+ π− J/ψ has been determined before using
the exclusive reconstruction method. Therefore, one can further obtain the absolute branching fraction of X(3872) →
π+ π− J/ψ. A precise determination of the latter is essential for elucidating the nature of the X(3872). In particular, if
the X(3872) is a tetraquark state, this branching fraction should be approximately 50% [381] while it is less than 10%
for a molecule [382, 383].
     In 2006 and 2017, using 232 × 106 and 772 × 106 BB̄ pairs, BaBar and Belle searched for the X(3872) in B
decays using a recoil mass technique [384, 385]. No clear signals were observed. The 90% C.L. upper limits on the
B(B+ → K + X(3872)) were set to be 3.2×10−4 and 2.6×10−4 at BaBar and Belle, respectively. BaBar updated this
measurement in 2020 [386]. In this analysis, if more than one B candidates were found in an event, all candidates
were retained to increase the efficiency and then two dedicated neural networks were applied to suppress backgrounds.
As a result, BaBar reported the measurement B(B+ → K + X(3872)) = (2.1 ± 0.6 ± 0.3) × 10−4 with a significance
of 3σ. This entails B(X(3872) → π+ π− J/ψ) = (4.1 ± 1.3)%, supporting the hypothesis of a molecular nature for
this resonance. Although B+ → K + X(3872) has been measured inclusively, the low significance (3σ) and 32%
relative uncertainty are still insufficient to firmly discriminate between competing theoretical models and limit the
precision of absolute branching fraction measurements for X(3872) decays. Using the large data sets, Belle and Belle
II propose a new inclusive study of B+ → K + X(3872) with an improved B-tagging method and a DeepSets-based
neural network [387] for background suppression. At a fixed background rejection level of 80%, the new method
achieves a signal efficiency of approximately 94%, a considerable improvement over the corresponding efficiency of
56% in the BaBar measurement [386]. After a selection criterion is applied to the output of the DeepSets network,
the resulting recoil mass distribution after subtracting the background from 711 fb−1 mock data at Belle is shown in
Fig. 27. The mock data consists of generic MC and signal MC samples. The generic MC samples include e+ e− →
B+ B− , e+ e− → B0 B̄0 , and e+ e− → qq̄ (q = u, d, s, c) events. The signal samples are generated according to the
PDG value of B(B+ → K + X(3872)) [24]. After subtracting the fitted background with a second-order exponential
function, the spectrum is fitted with double-Gaussian functions to describe the nine visible charmonium(-like) states:
ηc , J/ψ, χc0 , χc1 , χc2 , ηc (2S ), ψ′ , ψ(3770), and X(3872). The parameters of these signal shapes are fixed to those
obtained from independent signal MC fits. The statistical significance of the X(3872) signal exceeds 5σ, as illustrated
in Fig. 27. Belle and Belle II anticipate that this analysis will improve the precision of B(B+ → K + X(3872)) and
thereby contribute to understanding the nature of the X(3872).

4.2.6. Observation of T cc̄ s̄1 (4000)

In the meantime, it is claimed in theoretical work [389] that both line shapes from BESIII and LHCb can be reconciled
within a particular coupled-channel scheme as governed by the same pole of the amplitude. The existence of a near-
threshold state in this energy region is put in question in Ref. [390], where the interaction in the system is found to be
not strong enough to produce a bound state or resonance but sufficient to produce a large accumulation of strength at
the threshold. Yet, the most popular theoretical interpretations for the Zcs include its compact tetraquark assignment
[391] or a hadronic molecule picture [392–396], both predicting the existence of a partner state in the same mass
range. In the compact tetraquark model, the observed state Zcs (3985) and its predicted partner Zcs (4003) mirror the
situation observed for the X(3872) and Zc (3900) in the non-strange sector [391]. In the molecular model, the existence
of the Zcs (3985) entails the existence of its spin partner Zcs ′
                                                                   near the D̄∗s D∗ threshold [392–395]. Meanwhile, an
alternative scenario, also consistent with the existing BESIII data but requiring a certain fine tuning of the parameters
of the interaction, is additionally discussed in Ref. [395] where no partner state Zcs     ′
                                                                                               appears. Therefore, further
experimental studies are essential to clarify the properties of the Zcs family. One important approach is a search
for the corresponding isospin partners of these charged states. Recently, the BESIII Collaboration reported evidence
for a neutral partner, Zcs (3985)0 , in the D+s D∗− and D∗+                                                 0

4.2.7. Observation of X(3960)
     The state presently known as χc0 (3915) (originally named X(3915)) was first observed in the ωJ/ψ final state
by the Belle Collaboration [398–401]. Its properties are difficult to accommodate within conventional charmonium
assignments, such as χc0 (2P) or χc0 (3P), since decays of a conventional χc0 state into ωJ/ψ are expected to be OZI
suppressed. This tension is further reinforced by the long-standing absence of observed decays into open-charm fi-
nal states such as D(∗) D̄(∗) , together challenging a simple cc̄ or cc̄qq̄ (q = u, d) interpretation [173, 402–405]. In
Ref. [406], the χc0 (3915) is proposed as the lightest cc̄s s̄ state. The QCD sum-rule calculations favour its interpreta-
tion as a tetraquark, either with a cc̄qq̄ (q = u, d) or a cc̄s s̄ quark configuration [407]. It was conjectured in Ref. [408]
that the χc0 (3915) could be the helicity-0 realisation of a J PC = 2++ tensor charmonium-like state — a suitable
candidate χc2 (3930) was indeed previously reported by both Belle [409] and BaBar Collaborations [410]. However,
the proposed scenario called for the helicity-0 dominance in the two-photon decay, which was at odds with the well
established property of conventional tensor mesons [411] but could potentially take place for an exotic state. This hy-
pothesis was scrutinized in Ref. [412], where the corresponding tensor D∗ D̄∗ molecular state was treated as a HQSS
partner of the X(3872), and helicity-2 dominance in the two-photon decay was found to take place for it as well. In
2020, the χc0 (3930) state was observed by the LHCb Collaboration in the D+ D− invariant-mass spectrum [210, 211].
Its measured mass, width, and spin–parity quantum numbers are compatible with those of the χc0 (3915) resonance
previously observed in the ωJ/ψ channel, suggesting that the two structures could correspond to the same underlying
state. Some LQCD studies predict a narrow 0++ resonance just below the D+s D−s threshold, with a strong coupling to
D+s D−s and a much weaker coupling to DD̄, which may be related to the narrow χc0 (3915)/χc0 (3930) observed experi-
mentally [413]. In parallel, phenomenological studies have also proposed a D+s D−s molecular interpretation [414–422].
Meanwhile, the results of other LQCD calculations demonstrate no evidence for bound-state or resonance singular-
ities in the energy region between the χc0 (1P) state and the D s D̄ s threshold [423–425], so further investigations are
necessary to clarify the situation.
    Taken together, the above results point at the presence of a resonant structure in the vicinity of the D+s D−s threshold
in the corresponding invariant-mass spectrum. LHCb has performed a full amplitude analysis of the B+ → D+s D−s K +
decay using the complete Run 1 and Run 2 datasets [426]. The invariant-mass projections in M(D+s D−s ), M(D+s K + ),
and M(D−s K + ) are shown in Fig. 29. A pronounced structure near the D+s D−s threshold, modeled with a one-channel
Flatté-like lineshape including only the D+s D−s mode, is identified as the X(3960) state with a significance exceeding
                                                                          42

              12 σ and quantum numbers determined to be J PC = 0++ . The measured mass and width of the X(3960), listed in
              Table 8, are consistent with those of the χc0 (3930) within 3 σ [210, 211]. Assuming that X(3960) and χc0 (3930)
              correspond to the same state, an additional D+ D− coupled channel is introduced in the Flatté parameterization. The
              fit results remain compatible with the one-channel model, and the partial width for X(3960) → D+s D−s is found to be
              significantly larger than that for X(3960) → D+ D− . Since producing an s s̄ pair from the vacuum is suppressed relative
              to uū or dd̄, this behavior suggests that the X(3960) has a substantial cc̄s s̄ component, indicating a possible exotic
              hadronic structure. Further experimental studies are essential to clarify the nature of the D+s D−s threshold enhancement.
              In particular, measuring the relative branching fractions to the D(s) D̄(s) and ωJ/ψ channels in a different production
              environment, such as two-photon fusion processes at Belle II, would provide valuable complementary information. In
              this direction, a study of the B− → K − ωJ/ψ decay was performed in Ref. [427], where distinct features in the ωJ/ψ
              mass spectrum were predicted under the assumption that the X(3960) is a molecular state predominantly composed of
              DD̄ and D s D̄ s components.
                  In the M(D+s D−s ) spectrum, a dip is observed near 4140 MeV/c2 . This feature can be described either by intro-
              ducing a J PC = 0++ state, X0 (4140), which interferes destructively with the nonresonant 0++ component and the
              X(3960) amplitude, or by invoking coupled-channel effects associated with the J/ψϕ ↔ D+s D−s rescattering. The spin
              parity J PC = 0++ of X0 (4140) is incompatible with the 1++ quantum number of the X(4140) resonance (also known
              as χc1 (4140)) observed in the J/ψϕ final state [24]. Given that the significance of X0 (4140) is only 3.5 σ, additional
              measurements will be crucial for establishing its existence and understanding the underlying phenomena. Alternative
              theoretical hypotheses for the measured states should also be scrutinized. For example, if the χc0 (3930) is assigned as
              a D s D̄ s molecule, then the X(4140) can be understood as its spin-partner D∗s D̄∗s molecular state [428], and the events
              currently prescribed to the X(3960) could potentially be identified with the tail from the χc0 (3930) signal.

              4.2.8. Observation of χc1 (4010)
                  A simultaneous amplitude analysis of the decays B+ → D∗± D∓ K + is performed for the first time using the full
              Run 1 and Run 2 pp collision data collected by the LHCb experiment [212]. The analysis exploits the C-parity
              relations between contributions of charmonium(-like) resonances appearing in the two charge-conjugate channels. As
              shown in Fig. 30, four charmonium(-like) states decaying into D∗± D∓ are observed: ηc (3945), hc (4000), χc1 (4010),
              and hc (4300), with their J PC quantum numbers determined to be 0−+ , 1+− , 1++ , and 1+− , respectively. The ηc (3945) is
              compatible with the previously reported X(3940) state [429, 430], while the remaining three structures are observed
              for the first time. The observed χc1 (4010) has quantum numbers J PC = 1++ and is thus naturally associated with the
              χc1 charmonium sector. However, its mass does not fit naturally into the established pattern defined by the χc1 (3930)
              and χc1 (4140) states. Being located near the open-charm DD̄∗ threshold, the state is expected to be strongly affected
                                                                                                            43
by coupled-channel dynamics. Recent studies, including a reanalysis of the LQCD energy levels reported in Ref. [431]
and a separate LQCD calculation in Ref. [432], suggest a rich interplay between the χc1 (2P) charmonium state and
the DD̄∗ channel. In the former case, it is found that a strong coupling to DD̄∗ may generate an additional 1++ pole
above threshold, with a mass around 4.0 GeV, which could be associated with the observed χc1 (4010) [422]. In the
latter study, mixing between the χc1 (2P) state and noninteracting DD̄∗ scattering levels is shown to shift finite-volume
energy levels, pushing one level below threshold while raising another above. A subsequent K-matrix analysis of
these spectra yields both a DD̄∗ bound state, naturally identified with the X(3872), and a resonance pole slightly
above 4.0 GeV with a width of approximately 40–60 MeV, compatible with the LHCb observation of the χc1 (4010).
Nevertheless, the nature of the χc1 (4010) is still not firmly established and requires further theoretical and experimental
clarification.
    In addition, the two resonant structures T c̄∗s̄00 (2870) and T c̄∗s̄10 (2900), previously observed in B+ → D+ D− K + de-
cays [210, 211], are incorporated into the B+ → D∗+ D− K + amplitude model to describe the enhancement seen in
Fig. 30(e), thereby confirming their presence in this new decay channel. The measured ratio of branching fractions
between B+ → D∗+ T c̄∗s̄00 (2870) and B+ → D∗+ T c̄∗s̄10 (2900) is determined to be 1.17 ± 0.31 ± 0.48. This value is larger
than the corresponding ratio 0.18 ± 0.05 observed in B+ → D+ T c̄∗s̄0,1      0
                                                                                decays, and may provide further insight into the
                                   ∗0
production mechanisms of the T c̄ s̄0,1 states, if their existence is firmly established.

4.3. Pentaquarks

4.3.1. Theory overview
      An important milestone in the study of exotic baryons beyond the conventional three-quark configuration was the
discovery by the LHCb Collaboration in 2015 of the charmonium pentaquark candidates Pcc̄ (4450) and Pcc̄ (4380)
in the J/ψp invariant mass spectrum [433]. With increased statistics from Run-2 data, LHCb subsequently reported
in 2019 [434], in the decay process Λb → J/ψpK − , the observation of a lighter and narrow state Pcc̄ (4312) and
resolved the original Pcc̄ (4450) structure into two distinct narrow states, Pcc̄ (4440) and Pcc̄ (4457). Remarkably, the
existence of such hidden-charm pentaquark states with a predominantly molecular nature had been predicted nearly
a decade earlier [435, 436], in qualitative agreement with the subsequently observed spectrum (see also Ref. [437]).
Since their discovery, the nature of the three narrow Pc states has been the subject of intense theoretical debate. In
particular, several coupled-channel approaches consistent with HQSS — some of them including one-pion exchange,
— in which the Pc states are interpreted as Σ(∗)   c D̄
                                                        (∗)
                                                            real or virtual bound state hadronic molecules, successfully
reproduce the observed structures, allow for spin–parity assignments, and predict the possible existence of a narrow
Pcc̄ (4380) state [438–445]. The spectrum and decay patterns of the Pc states —– including those of their spin partners
— within the BOEFT approach have been recently analyzed in Ref. [446]. Some studies suggest that structures such
as Pcc̄ (4312) or Pcc̄ (4457) could be explained by “triangle diagrams”, where intermediate particles are exchanged
in such a way that they create an artificial peak in data without the need for a real pentaquark [447]. Consistency
limitations of some of these states being merely a kinematic effect, rather than corresponding to a real resonance, were
discussed since the earliest observations by LHCb [448, 449]. On the other hand, first LQCD studies of hidden-charm
pentaquarks have started to emerge, providing first-principles insights into the Σc D̄(∗) systems and their possible bound
or resonant nature [450] and the minor role played by the J/ψN and ηc N channels [451, 452]. For the pentaquark
studies employing the QCD sum rules technique see, for example, Refs. [453–455].
    Compact pentaquark interpretations based on constituent quark models and diquark correlations have been exten-
sively explored following the LHCb discoveries. In these approaches, the Pc states are described as tightly bound
five-quark configurations, often organized in diquark–diquark–antiquark or diquark–triquark structures, and their
spectra can be reproduced by effective chromomagnetic Hamiltonians or quark potential models — see, for exam-
ple, Refs. [457–462]. However, such models typically predict a rich spectrum of compact pentaquark states, many
of which have not been observed experimentally. While compact quark-model descriptions provide useful qualitative
insights, the proximity of the observed Pc states to Σ(∗)
                                                      c D̄
                                                           (∗)
                                                               thresholds, together with coupled-channel and LQCD re-
sults, strongly suggests that long-range hadronic dynamics plays a dominant role, making a predominantly molecular
interpretation highly plausible. There are also studies of hidden-charm pentaquarks using the QCD sum rule method
(see, for example, Refs. [463, 464]), although the associated theoretical uncertainties are larger than those in EFT-

based approaches. Reviews containing various theoretical interpretations of the dynamics and structure of these exotic
states can be found in Refs. [119, 465–467].
     All theoretical frameworks mentioned above can naturally accommodate hidden-charm strange resonant structures
with the quark content cc̄sqq, and a vast literature on this subject exists. Here we cite only a few representative works
such as Refs. [435, 436, 459, 468–484]. As will be discussed in detail below, two states, Pcc̄s (4338)0 and Pcc̄s (4459)0 ,
have been reported by the LHCb Collaboration in the J/ψ Λ invariant-mass distributions of different bottom-hadron
decays. Within the molecular picture, these exotic baryons arise from coupled-channel dynamics of the D̄(∗) Ξ(′,∗)    c
systems, described by effective Lagrangians respecting HQSS and SU(3) light-flavor symmetry. A distinctive feature
of this sector is that, in the heavy-quark limit, the light degrees of freedom couple to spin zero in the Ξc baryon as
in the Λc , while they couple to spin one in the Ξ′c as in the non-strange Σc . As a consequence, the D̄Ξc –D̄ s Λc and
D̄∗ Ξc –D̄∗s Λc channels, connected by strong transition potentials, cooperate to generate a larger attraction than the
corresponding D̄Σc –D̄Λc and D̄∗ Σc –D̄∗ Λc channels involved in the formation of the strangenessless Pc states, since in
the latter case the transition potentials between these channels are suppressed [470, 476].




                                                                                              45
Figure 31: Distributions of J/ψΛ and Λ p̄ invariant masses in B− → J/ψΛ p̄ decay. Fit results to data using the nominal model are superimposed.

line in the m(J/ψΛ) invariant mass distribution [456].


4.3.2. Observation of Pcc̄s (4338)0
    Over the past decade, the LHCb Collaboration has reported the observation and evidence of several pentaquark
candidates. More recently, evidence for a new pentaquark state Pcc̄ (4338) has been observed in the B0s → J/ψp p̄
decay with a mass of (4337 +7      +2         2                     +26 +14
                                −4 −2 ) MeV/c and width of (29 −12 −14 ) MeV [485, 486]. In addition, evidence for a
                                            0
strange pentaquark candidate Pcc̄s (4459) with quark content cc̄uds has been found in the J/ψΛ system produced in
Ξ−b → J/ψΛK − decays [487]. Experimentally, pentaquark candidates are often observed near the thresholds for the
production of conventional baryon–meson pairs. For instance, the previously reported P+cc̄ states are located close to
the Σ+c D̄0 and Σ+c D̄∗0 thresholds [433, 434], while the P0cc̄s state appears near the Ξ0c D̄∗0 threshold [487]. The decay
B− → J/ψΛ p̄ provides a unique laboratory to search simultaneously for the P̄−cc̄ and P0cc̄s pentaquark candidates in
the J/ψ p̄ and J/ψΛ systems, respectively. Owing to its large available phase space, this decay allows sensitivity to
pentaquark states located near various baryon–meson thresholds, such as Λ+c D̄0 for P+cc̄ states and Λ+c D−s or Ξ+c D− for
strange pentaquark candidates.
    Using the full Run 1 and Run 2 proton–proton collision data sets, the LHCb Collaboration performed a full
amplitude analysis of the B− → J/ψΛ p̄ decay [456], reconstructing approximately 4400 high-purity signal events. In
the nominal fit to data, a new narrow structure is observed in the J/ψΛ invariant-mass spectrum with a significance
exceeding 15 σ, as shown in Fig. 31. The observed state, denoted as Pcc̄s (4338)0 , has a mass and width measured to
be M = (4338.2 ± 0.7 ± 0.4) MeV/c2 and Γ = (7.0 ± 1.2 ± 1.3) MeV, respectively, with quantum numbers J P = 1/2−
preferred. The Pcc̄s (4338)0 state contains at least five valence quarks, c̄csud, and is located near the Ξ+c D− baryon–
meson threshold, a feature that is highly relevant for interpretations of its internal structure.

4.3.3. Evidence of the Pcc̄s (4459)0 in Υ(1S , 2S ) decays
    In 2021, LHCb reported an evidence (3.1σ) of a pentaquark candidate state with a udscc̄ quark component,

Λc D s system. The accessible phase space of the Λ+c D−s invariant mass, ranging from 4235 to 4632 MeV/c2 , covers

fraction of this decay relative to Λ0b → Λ+c D−s is measured to be

5. Future prospects

5.1. The Belle II experiment

    Belle II is the only currently operating B-factory experiment in the world that has been designed for precise
measurements of weak interaction parameters, study exotic hadrons, and search for new phenomena beyond the
Standard Model of particle physics. It started to record physics data in 2019. In June 2022, SuperKEKB achieved a
new instantaneous luminosity world record of 4.7 × 1034 cm−2 s−1 . From 2019 to 2022, Belle II accumulated a 428
fb−1 data sample (run 1) [37] and, from 2024 to 2025, it accumulated a 165 fb−1 data sample (run 2). The luminosity
projection plot for the coming years is shown in Fig. 34 [38]. The integrated luminosity at Belle ll is expected to
achieve 4 ab−1 and 10 ab−1 in 2029 and 2034, respectively. With and without the Quadrupole-Corrector-Sextupole
(QCS) upgrade, the instantaneous luminosity can achieve 6.2×1035 cm−2 s−1 and 3.1×1035 cm−2 s−1 , and the integrated
luminosity can achieve 50 ab−1 and 34 ab−1 by 2043.
    There are four ways to explore the bottomonium-like states at Belle II: (i) direct e+ e− annihilations, (ii) initial state
radiation (ISR) processes [494], (iii) hadronic transitions, and (iv) radiative transitions. In e+ e− collisions, the C.M.
energy can be set at some specific energy points to search for possible new states and measure the masses and widths
of known resonances. The Υ(10753) was discovered in the π+ π− Υ(nS ) final states using direct e+ e− collision data
and the ISR process from 10.52 to 11.02 GeV at Belle [23]. The hadronic and radiative transitions are also effective
ways to search for new bottomonium-like states. Noticeably, the Zb (10610) and Zb (10650) were observed in the pion
transition from Υ(10860) and Υ(11020) at Belle [22]. The bottomonium ground state ηb (1S ) was observed in the
radiative decay of Υ(3S ) [495]. At Belle II, an energy scan from the BB̄ threshold up to the highest possible energy of
11.24 GeV, with approximately 10 fb−1 per point and 10 MeV step, is of great interest in the search for new excited Υ
and Yb states [496]. If new bottomonium(-like) states are observed, more data will be collected at the corresponding
energy points to perform a detailed study of radiative and hadronic transitions to search for Xb and other Zb states.
    Below, we discuss future prospects for the Zb /WbJ , Xb , and Yb states in more detail.

    • Zb /WbJ states:

5.2. The LHCb experiment
    The achievements of the LHCb physics program in Run 1–2 have already revealed a rich landscape of exotic
hadrons, yet the current understanding remains incomplete. Following Upgrade I, the LHCb detector operates at
an instantaneous luminosity approximately five times higher than in Run 2 and now employs a fully software-based
trigger in place of the former hardware system [41]. This enables real-time event reconstruction and roughly doubles
the trigger efficiency for fully hadronic final states, providing a foundation for both the discovery of new exotic states
and major progress in long-standing questions related to previously observed structures and decay modes. By the end
of 2026, Run 3 has a target integrated luminosity of approximately 23 fb−1 , a goal that is already close to being met
with more than 22 fb−1 recorded so far. An intermediate Upgrade Ib, scheduled between Runs 3 and 4, will focus
on consolidating detector performance without a further increase in instantaneous luminosity. By the end of Run 4,
the total integrated luminosity is expected to reach around 50 fb−1 , corresponding to an order-of-magnitude increase
in heavy-flavor yields compared to Run 1–2 (see Fig. 35 and Table 9). A major Upgrade II is foreseen for the High-
Luminosity LHC era [497], enabling operation at approximately seven times the current luminosity and aiming for a
total integrated luminosity of about 300 fb−1 over the lifetime of the program. This dataset will provide a significant
step forward for precision measurements and detailed studies of exotic hadrons.

Precise measurement of the exotic candidates. For the well-established exotic state X(3872), larger data samples
will enable studies of additional decay modes and a more precise determination of its branching fractions. In ra-
diative decays, the photon reconstruction efficiency at LHCb is intrinsically low; however, the statistics foreseen in
Upgrade II are expected to compensate for this limitation, allowing significantly improved measurements of radiative
decay rates and, consequently, tighter constraints on the internal structure of the state. Another approach to probe
the nature of the X(3872), not discussed in detail in this review, is the study of its prompt-production cross section at
the LHC in comparison with conventional charmonium states. The System for Measuring Overlap with Gas 2 [502]
further extends these studies to additional colliding systems (for example p–He, p–Ne, p–Ar), providing complemen-
tary information on production mechanisms. Compared with X(3872), many other exotic candidates — such as the
T cc̄1 (4430) — remain less well explored. In some cases, measurements from different experiments are inconsistent; in
others, the uncertainties are too large to discriminate between theoretical models, or the reported features can not be
accommodated by existing models. Clarifying their properties, or even establishing their existence, requires analyses
based on larger data samples. Amplitude analyses with larger data samples are essential to precisely determine their
resonance parameters, thereby providing crucial input to clarify their underlying nature (see for example Fig. 36).

Search for new exotic states. Multiquark candidates are often found near thresholds of hadron–hadron systems,
motivating dedicated searches in regions such as the Λc D̄ s and DD̄ thresholds. These processes typically involve
multi-body final states and low reconstruction efficiency, making large data samples essential. With the increased
instantaneous luminosity and upgraded detector performance in Run 3 and Run 4, LHCb is expected to collect samples
of order millions for b-hadron decays to final states containing J/ψ or ψ(2S ) → µ+ µ− , and tens of thousands of
events for decays to double open-charm final states (for example, DD̄, Λ+c D̄). The Run 3 and Run 4 data will also
enable searches for doubly-charmed exotic states and fully-charmed tetraquark candidates, including investigations of
structures in D(∗) D(∗) and J/ψJ/ψ systems. Some expected data samples and key decay modes are listed in Table 10.
These statistics will broaden the experimental reach for studies of threshold structures and offer a possibility to search
for tetraquark and pentaquark candidates in these modes. Related configurations are expected in the bottom sector.
LHCb is pursuing searches for open-bottom (bqqq), hidden-bottom (bb̄qq), and doubly-bottom (bbq̄q̄) candidates, as
well as their pentaquark analogues, in prompt production. This program will continue throughout Run 3, Run 4, and
into the HL-LHC era. Building on the established searches for tetraquark and pentaquark candidates, it is natural to
extend the exploration to dibaryons, which represent the next relevant class of multiquark systems. Such searches are
included in the current and future LHCb physics program.

5.3. Discussion on theoretical prospects
    The discovery and rapid proliferation of exotic hadronic states require both updates to traditional theoretical meth-
ods and the development of new frameworks appropriate to the present situation. In this subsection, we briefly review
the most developed and successful theoretical approaches to ordinary and exotic hadrons and outline prospects for
their future progress.
                                                                        51

                                                                                          LHCb 3 fb-1
